========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, um… I came in because I just can’t seem to be still. It’s like I got a motor running inside me all day, like my body is always telling me to move, even when I’m supposed to be sitting quiet.

Like at church, I try to sit there and listen, but after a little bit I feel trapped in the seat. Same thing here at the clinic, waiting around, or even just being at home. I’ll tell myself, “Just sit down, just relax,” but I can’t really do it. I gotta get up, walk around, mess with something, do something with my hands, anything. It feels real uncomfortable, like pressure building up inside me if I don’t move. That’s the main reason I’m here, because it’s been bothering me a lot.

Yeah, I mean… I notice it most in places where I’m expected to sit still, like church, here at the clinic, or even at home when I’m supposed to just sit down and relax. Even if nothing is really happening, I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Okay, just stay put,” but then that restless feeling builds up and I end up moving around. So yeah, it’s not just one place—it follows me around. It’s real uncomfortable.

Yeah, yeah, I do. It’s not always because I’m worried about something. That’s what makes it kind of frustrating, because sometimes nothing bad is happening, nobody’s bothering me, I’m not sitting there thinking about some problem or anything like that… but my body still feels like it’s running.

Like at church, I might actually be trying to pay attention, just sitting there, and then after a little while it’s like I feel trapped in the seat. Same kind of thing here at the clinic, just waiting, or even at home where there’s no reason I should feel worked up. I’ll be sitting there and it’s like there’s a motor inside me that won’t shut off, and I have to get up, walk around, or do something with my hands.

So it’s not only when I’m anxious. It’s just there a lot of the time, like my body doesn’t know how to be still.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s hard for me to separate it, you know? Because even if I’m sitting still on the outside for a minute, inside I still feel like I got that motor running. Like my body might be in the chair, but I don’t feel settled.

At church, for example, I’ll be sitting there trying to be respectful and listen, and I can feel this thing building in me, like I’m trapped in the seat. It’s not always like a thought racing about one thing, it’s more like I just can’t relax inside. Same here at the clinic, sitting and waiting, I start feeling like I need to get up, move around, touch something, do something with my hands. Even at home, where I should be able to just sit down and be comfortable, I still get that feeling like I can’t stay put.

So yeah, even before I move, there’s this restless feeling inside me. Like I’m wound up, and if I don’t get up or do something, it just gets more uncomfortable.

Um… unusually excited or energized? I don’t know if I’d call it that. I mean, I get restless and wound up, like I’ve been saying, but not really like I’m in some great mood or full of big energy where I feel different from myself.

As for thoughts racing… sometimes my mind doesn’t feel settled, but I’m not sure it’s like my thoughts are going so fast I can’t keep up with them. It’s more like I can’t relax, you know? I don’t know if I’d call it that. I mean, I get restless and wound up, like I’ve been saying, but not really like I’m in some great mood or full of big energy where I feel different from myself.\n\nAs for thoughts racing… sometimes my mind doesn’t feel settled, but I’m not sure it’s like my thoughts are going so fast I can’t keep up with them. It’s more like I can’t relax, you know? Like I feel uncomfortable sitting there. But “unusually excited” or my thoughts flying that fast… I can’t really say I’ve noticed that, no.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find it hard to focus or concentrate on things, like reading, watching TV, or following conversations, even when you're not moving around?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 36 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Sustained_Attention_Difficulty\nmanifestation: \"When folks are talking through ride times, pantry lists, or church plans, I listen good at first, but then my mind slips off and I miss pieces I really needed to hear.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I do. Even if I’m sitting still and not moving around, I can have a hard time keeping with what somebody’s saying. Like I’ll start out listening good, I really will, and I’ll be trying to follow it, but then somewhere in the middle my mind just kind of slips off.

It happens with regular stuff I need to know, too. Like if somebody’s talking through ride times — who’s picking up who, what time we’re leaving, where I’m supposed to be — I’ll hear the first part, and then I miss a piece, and then I’m confused later. Same with pantry lists, like if someone’s telling me what needs to be picked up or what’s already there, I’ll think I got it, but then I realize I missed some of it. Or church plans, when people are explaining what’s going on, what time something starts, who’s doing what… I’m listening at first, but after a bit I lose pieces I really needed.

And it’s frustrating because it’s not always like I’m walking around or doing something else. I can be right there looking at the person, trying to listen, and still it doesn’t all stay with me.

But “unusually excited” or my thoughts flying that fast… I can’t really say I’ve noticed that, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus or concentrate on things, like reading, watching TV, or following conversations, even when you're not moving around?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even if I’m sitting still and not moving around, I can have a hard time keeping with what somebody’s saying. Like I’ll start out listening good, I really will, and I’ll be trying to follow it, but then somewhere in the middle my mind just kind of slips off.\n\nIt happens with regular stuff I need to know, too. Like if somebody’s talking through ride times — who’s picking up who, what time we’re leaving, where I’m supposed to be — I’ll hear the first part, and then I miss a piece, and then I’m confused later. Same with pantry lists, like if someone’s telling me what needs to be picked up or what’s already there, I’ll think I got it, but then I realize I missed some of it. Or church plans, when people are explaining what’s going on, what time something starts, who’s doing what… I’m listening at first, but after a bit I lose pieces I really needed.\n\nAnd it’s frustrating because it’s not always like I’m walking around or doing something else. I can be right there looking at the person, trying to listen, and still it doesn’t all stay with me.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often find that you’re not paying close attention to details, or do you make careless mistakes because your mind is somewhere else?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 36 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure about that one. I mean, I can say I miss things sometimes, but whether it’s because I’m not paying close attention to details or because my mind is somewhere else… I don’t know if I could separate that out real clear.

Like, if somebody later says, “You didn’t notice that part,” or “That’s not what I said,” I might realize I missed something, but I don’t always know in the moment what happened. I don’t sit there thinking, “Oh, I’m ignoring the details.” It’s more after the fact that I might see I didn’t catch everything.

As far as careless mistakes, I don’t know. I don’t have a good example coming to mind right now. I’m not saying it never happens, but I haven’t really paid attention to it that way, like enough to say yes, that’s a big thing for me.

Even if I’m sitting still and not moving around, I can have a hard time keeping with what somebody’s saying. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… that’s actually kind of the main reason I came in, to be honest. It’s like I’ve got a motor running inside me all day. That’s the best way I can explain it. Even if I’m sitting there and I’m not supposed to be doing anything, inside I don’t feel still. I feel like I need to get up, or shift around, or go do something, or I’m just… stuck in my own skin, if that makes sense.

Like at church, if I have to sit through everything, I start feeling trapped. Not because anything bad is happening, but because sitting there that long feels almost unbearable sometimes. Same thing at the clinic, like waiting around, sitting in the chair, trying to just be patient — I can do it for a little while, but then I start feeling like I’ve got to stand up or move or find some reason to do something.

And at home too, it’s not like it only happens out in public. If I’m just sitting there and I’m supposed to relax, I don’t really relax. I’ll feel that pressure inside, like I need to get up. Even if I’m not physically running around, I still feel restless inside. That’s probably the biggest thing — I don’t feel settled.

supplies, and I feel like I keep letting everybody down even when I mean to help.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 2 months\n  manifestation: It has been this hard to manage for about two years and two months, ever since my older relative’s health changed and I had to take on more responsibilities.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 36 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S012\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus or concentrate on things, like reading, watching TV, or following conversations, even when you're not moving around?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even if I’m sitting still and not moving around, I can have a hard time keeping with what somebody’s saying. Like I’ll start out listening good, I really will, and I’ll be trying to follow it, but then somewhere in the middle my mind just kind of slips off.\n\nIt happens with regular stuff I need to know, too. Like if somebody’s talking through ride times — who’s picking up who, what time we’re leaving, where I’m supposed to be — I’ll hear the first part, and then I miss a piece, and then I’m confused later. Same with pantry lists, like if someone’s telling me what needs to be picked up or what’s already there, I’ll think I got it, but then I realize I missed some of it. Or church plans, when people are explaining what’s going on, what time something starts, who’s doing what… I’m listening at first, but after a bit I lose pieces I really needed.\n\nAnd it’s frustrating because it’s not always like I’m walking around or doing something else. I can be right there looking at the person, trying to listen, and still it doesn’t all stay with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find that you’re not paying close attention to details, or do you make careless mistakes because your mind is somewhere else?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure about that one. I mean, I can say I miss things sometimes, but whether it’s because I’m not paying close attention to details or because my mind is somewhere else… I don’t know if I could separate that out real clear.\n\nLike, if somebody later says, “You didn’t notice that part,” or “That’s not what I said,” I might realize I missed something, but I don’t always know in the moment what happened. I don’t sit there thinking, “Oh, I’m ignoring the details.” It’s more after the fact that I might see I didn’t catch everything.\n\nAs far as careless mistakes, I don’t know. I don’t have a good example coming to mind right now. I’m not saying it never happens, but I haven’t really paid attention to it that way, like enough to say yes, that’s a big thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you also have trouble staying focused or feel restless in settings like work, school, or when you’re out with friends, even if you’re not moving around?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually kind of the main reason I came in, to be honest. It’s like I’ve got a motor running inside me all day. That’s the best way I can explain it. Even if I’m sitting there and I’m not supposed to be doing anything, inside I don’t feel still. I feel like I need to get up, or shift around, or go do something, or I’m just… stuck in my own skin, if that makes sense.\n\nLike at church, if I have to sit through everything, I start feeling trapped. Not because anything bad is happening, but because sitting there that long feels almost unbearable sometimes. Same thing at the clinic, like waiting around, sitting in the chair, trying to just be patient — I can do it for a little while, but then I start feeling like I’ve got to stand up or move or find some reason to do something.\n\nAnd at home too, it’s not like it only happens out in public. If I’m just sitting there and I’m supposed to relax, I don’t really relax. I’ll feel that pressure inside, like I need to get up. Even if I’m not physically running around, I still feel restless inside. That’s probably the biggest thing — I don’t feel settled.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen did you first start noticing these feelings of restlessness and trouble focusing? Were they present when you were a child, or did they start more recently?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, it was definitely there when I was a kid. I don’t think this is something that just started recently. I mean, it feels worse now in some ways because I’m older and there’s more I’m supposed to handle, but when I think back, it was already happening when I was a boy in school.

Teachers were always moving my seat around. Like, not just once or twice — it felt like they were always trying to put me somewhere else because I couldn’t stay put. I’d be up before class was done, or I’d jump out of my seat when I wasn’t supposed to. Sometimes I’d talk out of turn, like I knew I was supposed to wait but it would just come out anyway, and then I’d get in trouble for it.

I remember being told to sit down, be quiet, stop moving, wait until class is over… that kind of thing. So, yeah, when you ask if it was there as a child, I’d say yes. It goes way back.

Same kind of thing here at the clinic, just waiting, or even at home where there’s no reason I should feel worked up. I’ll be sitting there and it’s like there’s a motor inside me that won’t shut off, and I have to get up, walk around, or do something with my hands.\n\nSo it’s not only when I’m anxious. It’s just there a lot of the time, like my body doesn’t know how to be still.\n\nDoctor: Do you also feel a sense of inner tension or mental restlessness, like your mind is racing or you just can’t relax inside, even if you’re not moving?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s hard for me to separate it, you know? Because even if I’m sitting still on the outside for a minute, inside I still feel like I got that motor running. Like my body might be in the chair, but I don’t feel settled.\n\nAt church, for example, I’ll be sitting there trying to be respectful and listen, and I can feel this thing building in me, like I’m trapped in the seat. It’s not always like a thought racing about one thing, it’s more like I just can’t relax inside. Same here at the clinic, sitting and waiting, I start feeling like I need to get up, move around, touch something, do something with my hands. Even at home, where I should be able to just sit down and be comfortable, I still get that feeling like I can’t stay put.\n\nSo yeah, even before I move, there’s this restless feeling inside me. Like I’m wound up, and if I don’t get up or do something, it just gets more uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually excited, energized, or like your thoughts were racing so fast you couldn’t keep up with them?\n\nPatient: Um… unusually excited or energized? I don’t know if I’d call it that. It’s more like I can’t relax, you know? Like I feel uncomfortable sitting there. I mean, I can say I miss things sometimes, but whether it’s because I’m not paying close attention to details or because my mind is somewhere else… I don’t know if I could separate that out real clear.\n\nLike, if somebody later says, “You didn’t notice that part,” or “That’s not what I said,” I might realize I missed something, but I don’t always know in the moment what happened. I don’t sit there thinking, “Oh, I’m ignoring the details.” It’s more after the fact that I might see I didn’t catch everything.\n\nAs far as careless mistakes, I don’t know. I don’t have a good example coming to mind right now. I’m not saying it never happens, but I haven’t really paid attention to it that way, like enough to say yes, that’s a big thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you also have trouble staying focused or feel restless in settings like work, school, or when you’re out with friends, even if you’re not moving around?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually kind of the main reason I came in, to be honest. It’s like I’ve got a motor running inside me all day. That’s the best way I can explain it. Even if I’m sitting there and I’m not supposed to be doing anything, inside I don’t feel still. I feel like I need to get up, or shift around, or go do something, or I’m just… stuck in my own skin, if that makes sense.\n\nLike at church, if I have to sit through everything, I start feeling trapped. Same thing at the clinic, like waiting around, sitting in the chair, trying to just be patient — I can do it for a little while, but then I start feeling like I’ve got to stand up or move or find some reason to do something.\n\nAnd at home too, it’s not like it only happens out in public. If I’m just sitting there and I’m supposed to relax, I don’t really relax. I’ll feel that pressure inside, like I need to get up. Even if I’m not physically running around, I still feel restless inside. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I do. It happens a lot when somebody is telling me something I’m supposed to hold onto for more than just a second.

Like if we’re talking about ride times — say somebody’s telling me, “Be ready at this time, then we’re going here, then we’re picking up so-and-so,” I’ll be listening at the start. I’m not trying to ignore them. I’ll be looking at them, nodding, thinking I’m getting it. But then somewhere in the middle my mind kind of slips off, and I don’t always notice right away. Then later I realize I missed the part I actually needed, like what time exactly or where I was supposed to be.

Same thing with pantry lists or church plans. If somebody’s going through a list of things, I might catch the first couple things, then it gets blurry. I’ll remember there was something about this person bringing that, or we’re supposed to meet somewhere, but I don’t have the full thing. Then I have to ask again, and I feel bad because I know they already told me.

So yeah, if it’s something that needs me to keep my attention on it for a while, especially listening to instructions or plans, I start out okay but I don’t stay locked in. My mind just kind of wanders off before I mean for it to.

I mean, it feels worse now in some ways because I’m older and there’s more I’m supposed to handle, but when I think back, it was already happening when I was a boy in school.\n\nTeachers were always moving my seat around. Like, not just once or twice — it felt like they were always trying to put me somewhere else because I couldn’t stay put. I’d be up before class was done, or I’d jump out of my seat when I wasn’t supposed to. Sometimes I’d talk out of turn, like I knew I was supposed to wait but it would just come out anyway, and then I’d get in trouble for it.\n\nI remember being told to sit down, be quiet, stop moving, wait until class is over… that kind of thing. So, yeah, when you ask if it was there as a child, I’d say yes. It goes way back.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have trouble staying focused or feel restless when you’re doing tasks that require you to keep your attention on something for a while, like reading, writing, or working on a project?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It happens a lot when somebody is telling me something I’m supposed to hold onto for more than just a second.\n\nLike if we’re talking about ride times — say somebody’s telling me, “Be ready at this time, then we’re going here, then we’re picking up so-and-so,” I’ll be listening at the start. I’m not trying to ignore them. I’ll be looking at them, nodding, thinking I’m getting it. But then somewhere in the middle my mind kind of slips off, and I don’t always notice right away. Then later I realize I missed the part I actually needed, like what time exactly or where I was supposed to be.\n\nSame thing with pantry lists or church plans. If somebody’s going through a list of things, I might catch the first couple things, then it gets blurry. I’ll remember there was something about this person bringing that, or we’re supposed to meet somewhere, but I don’t have the full thing. Then I have to ask again, and I feel bad because I know they already told me.\n\nSo yeah, if it’s something that needs me to keep my attention on it for a while, especially listening to instructions or plans, I start out okay but I don’t stay locked in. My mind just kind of wanders off before I mean for it to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, definitely. I mean, I’m not working right now, so I can’t really say like “at work” currently, but in other places, yes. It follows me around. It’s not just one place.

That’s honestly a big part of why I came in. I feel like I’ve got this motor running inside me all day. Like even if I’m sitting still on the outside, inside I don’t feel still. I feel like I need to get up, or move, or do something with myself. If I’m at church and I have to sit there for a long stretch, I start feeling trapped, like I’m stuck in the seat. I’ll be shifting around, thinking about standing up, looking for a reason to go do something, even if I know I’m supposed to just sit and listen.

Same thing at the clinic, like waiting in a chair. I can sit there for a little bit, but then it starts building up in me. It’s not even that I’m trying to be rude or impatient. It just feels like my body is saying, “Get up, get up, get up.” And at home it happens too, which is what makes it frustrating, because home is where you’re supposed to relax. But I’ll be sitting there and still feel like I can’t settle, like I need to pop up and do something, even if there’s nothing that really needs doing.

So yeah, around people, out places, or even just at home by myself, that restless feeling is there a lot.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, thank you for coming in. Can you tell me what's been bothering you or what brings you here today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I came in because I just can’t seem to be still. It’s like I got a motor running inside me all day, like my body is always telling me to move, even when I’m supposed to be sitting quiet.\n\nLike at church, I try to sit there and listen, but after a little bit I feel trapped in the seat. Same thing here at the clinic, waiting around, or even just being at home. I’ll tell myself, “Just sit down, just relax,” but I can’t really do it. I gotta get up, walk around, mess with something, do something with my hands, anything. It feels real uncomfortable, like pressure building up inside me if I don’t move. That’s the main reason I’m here, because it’s been bothering me a lot.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned feeling restless in church, at the clinic, and at home. Do you also feel this way in other places, like at work or school, or when you're out with friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I notice it most in places where I’m expected to sit still, like church, here at the clinic, or even at home when I’m supposed to just sit down and relax. It’s like my body doesn’t know how to just be quiet. I feel kind of trapped in the chair, like there’s this motor inside me running all day, and after a little bit I have to get up or do something with my hands.\n\nEven if nothing is really happening, I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Okay, just stay put,” but then that restless feeling builds up and I end up moving around. So yeah, it’s not just one place—it follows me around. It’s real uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this restlessness even when you're not anxious or worried about anything?\n\nPatient: Yeah, yeah, I do. It’s not always because I’m worried about something. That’s what makes it kind of frustrating, because sometimes nothing bad is happening, nobody’s bothering me, I’m not sitting there thinking about some problem or anything like that… but my body still feels like it’s running.\n\nLike at church, I might actually be trying to pay attention, just sitting there, and then after a little while it’s like I feel trapped in the seat. Same kind of thing here at the clinic, just waiting, or even at home where there’s no reason I should feel worked up. I’ll be sitting there and it’s like there’s a motor inside me that won’t shut off, and I have to get up, walk around, or do something with my hands.\n\nSo it’s not only when I’m anxious. It’s just there a lot of the time, like my body doesn’t know how to be still.\n\nDoctor: Do you also feel a sense of inner tension or mental restlessness, like your mind is racing or you just can’t relax inside, even if you’re not moving?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s hard for me to separate it, you know? Because even if I’m sitting still on the outside for a minute, inside I still feel like I got that motor running. Like my body might be in the chair, but I don’t feel settled.\n\nAt church, for example, I’ll be sitting there trying to be respectful and listen, and I can feel this thing building in me, like I’m trapped in the seat. It’s not always like a thought racing about one thing, it’s more like I just can’t relax inside. Same here at the clinic, sitting and waiting, I start feeling like I need to get up, move around, touch something, do something with my hands. Even at home, where I should be able to just sit down and be comfortable, I still get that feeling like I can’t stay put.\n\nSo yeah, even before I move, there’s this restless feeling inside me. Like I’m wound up, and if I don’t get up or do something, it just gets more uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually excited, energized, or like your thoughts were racing so fast you couldn’t keep up with them?\n\nPatient: Um… unusually excited or energized? I don’t know if I’d call it that. I mean, I get restless and wound up, like I’ve been saying, but not really like I’m in some great mood or full of big energy where I feel different from myself.\n\nAs for thoughts racing… sometimes my mind doesn’t feel settled, but I’m not sure it’s like my thoughts are going so fast I can’t keep up with them. It’s more like I can’t relax, you know? Like I feel uncomfortable sitting there. But “unusually excited” or my thoughts flying that fast… I can’t really say I’ve noticed that, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus or concentrate on things, like reading, watching TV, or following conversations, even when you're not moving around?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even if I’m sitting still and not moving around, I can have a hard time keeping with what somebody’s saying. Like I’ll start out listening good, I really will, and I’ll be trying to follow it, but then somewhere in the middle my mind just kind of slips off.\n\nIt happens with regular stuff I need to know, too. Like if somebody’s talking through ride times — who’s picking up who, what time we’re leaving, where I’m supposed to be — I’ll hear the first part, and then I miss a piece, and then I’m confused later. Same with pantry lists, like if someone’s telling me what needs to be picked up or what’s already there, I’ll think I got it, but then I realize I missed some of it. Or church plans, when people are explaining what’s going on, what time something starts, who’s doing what… I’m listening at first, but after a bit I lose pieces I really needed.\n\nAnd it’s frustrating because it’s not always like I’m walking around or doing something else. I can be right there looking at the person, trying to listen, and still it doesn’t all stay with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find that you’re not paying close attention to details, or do you make careless mistakes because your mind is somewhere else?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure about that one. I mean, I can say I miss things sometimes, but whether it’s because I’m not paying close attention to details or because my mind is somewhere else… I don’t know if I could separate that out real clear.\n\nLike, if somebody later says, “You didn’t notice that part,” or “That’s not what I said,” I might realize I missed something, but I don’t always know in the moment what happened. I don’t sit there thinking, “Oh, I’m ignoring the details.” It’s more after the fact that I might see I didn’t catch everything.\n\nAs far as careless mistakes, I don’t know. I don’t have a good example coming to mind right now. I’m not saying it never happens, but I haven’t really paid attention to it that way, like enough to say yes, that’s a big thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you also have trouble staying focused or feel restless in settings like work, school, or when you’re out with friends, even if you’re not moving around?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually kind of the main reason I came in, to be honest. It’s like I’ve got a motor running inside me all day. That’s the best way I can explain it. Even if I’m sitting there and I’m not supposed to be doing anything, inside I don’t feel still. I feel like I need to get up, or shift around, or go do something, or I’m just… stuck in my own skin, if that makes sense.\n\nLike at church, if I have to sit through everything, I start feeling trapped. Not because anything bad is happening, but because sitting there that long feels almost unbearable sometimes. Same thing at the clinic, like waiting around, sitting in the chair, trying to just be patient — I can do it for a little while, but then I start feeling like I’ve got to stand up or move or find some reason to do something.\n\nAnd at home too, it’s not like it only happens out in public. If I’m just sitting there and I’m supposed to relax, I don’t really relax. I’ll feel that pressure inside, like I need to get up. Even if I’m not physically running around, I still feel restless inside. That’s probably the biggest thing — I don’t feel settled.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing these feelings of restlessness and trouble focusing? Were they present when you were a child, or did they start more recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it was definitely there when I was a kid. I don’t think this is something that just started recently. I mean, it feels worse now in some ways because I’m older and there’s more I’m supposed to handle, but when I think back, it was already happening when I was a boy in school.\n\nTeachers were always moving my seat around. Like, not just once or twice — it felt like they were always trying to put me somewhere else because I couldn’t stay put. I’d be up before class was done, or I’d jump out of my seat when I wasn’t supposed to. Sometimes I’d talk out of turn, like I knew I was supposed to wait but it would just come out anyway, and then I’d get in trouble for it.\n\nI remember being told to sit down, be quiet, stop moving, wait until class is over… that kind of thing. So, yeah, when you ask if it was there as a child, I’d say yes. It goes way back.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have trouble staying focused or feel restless when you’re doing tasks that require you to keep your attention on something for a while, like reading, writing, or working on a project?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It happens a lot when somebody is telling me something I’m supposed to hold onto for more than just a second.\n\nLike if we’re talking about ride times — say somebody’s telling me, “Be ready at this time, then we’re going here, then we’re picking up so-and-so,” I’ll be listening at the start. I’m not trying to ignore them. I’ll be looking at them, nodding, thinking I’m getting it. But then somewhere in the middle my mind kind of slips off, and I don’t always notice right away. Then later I realize I missed the part I actually needed, like what time exactly or where I was supposed to be.\n\nSame thing with pantry lists or church plans. If somebody’s going through a list of things, I might catch the first couple things, then it gets blurry. I’ll remember there was something about this person bringing that, or we’re supposed to meet somewhere, but I don’t have the full thing. Then I have to ask again, and I feel bad because I know they already told me.\n\nSo yeah, if it’s something that needs me to keep my attention on it for a while, especially listening to instructions or plans, I start out okay but I don’t stay locked in. My mind just kind of wanders off before I mean for it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have trouble staying focused or feel restless in other settings, like when you’re at work or when you’re interacting with friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… waiting my turn is a big one for me. Lines, especially, or sitting at the doctor’s office waiting to be called back — that gets me worked up fast.

I’ll tell myself, “Just stand here, just wait, everybody has to wait,” but my body doesn’t really listen. I start stepping around, shifting from one foot to the other, looking around, checking the door, watching who gets called before me. And then I’ll start asking, like, “How much longer do you think it’ll be?” or “Am I next?” even when I know they probably already told me to wait.

It’s embarrassing sometimes because I know I look impatient. I’m not trying to make anybody’s job harder. It just builds up in me, like I feel stuck there. At the doctor’s office, if I’m waiting too long, I’ll get up and walk around, go outside for a minute, come back in, ask again. And there have been times where I’ve just left when I really should have stayed, because I couldn’t take sitting there or standing there anymore.

So yeah, waiting my turn is hard. It’s not just annoying to me — it makes me feel all stirred up inside, like I have to do something right then.

Like even if I’m sitting still on the outside, inside I don’t feel still. I feel like I need to get up, or move, or do something with myself. If I’m at church and I have to sit there for a long stretch, I start feeling trapped, like I’m stuck in the seat. I’ll be shifting around, thinking about standing up, looking for a reason to go do something, even if I know I’m supposed to just sit and listen.\n\nSame thing at the clinic, like waiting in a chair. I can sit there for a little bit, but then it starts building up in me. It’s not even that I’m trying to be rude or impatient. It just feels like my body is saying, “Get up, get up, get up.” And at home it happens too, which is what makes it frustrating, because home is where you’re supposed to relax. But I’ll be sitting there and still feel like I can’t settle, like I need to pop up and do something, even if there’s nothing that really needs doing.\n\nSo yeah, around people, out places, or even just at home by myself, that restless feeling is there a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others when they’re talking, acting without thinking about the consequences, or having trouble waiting your turn in situations like conversations or lines?\n\nPatient: Yeah… waiting my turn is a big one for me. Lines, especially, or sitting at the doctor’s office waiting to be called back — that gets me worked up fast.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Just stand here, just wait, everybody has to wait,” but my body doesn’t really listen. I start stepping around, shifting from one foot to the other, looking around, checking the door, watching who gets called before me. And then I’ll start asking, like, “How much longer do you think it’ll be?” or “Am I next?” even when I know they probably already told me to wait.\n\nIt’s embarrassing sometimes because I know I look impatient. I’m not trying to make anybody’s job harder. It just builds up in me, like I feel stuck there. At the doctor’s office, if I’m waiting too long, I’ll get up and walk around, go outside for a minute, come back in, ask again. And there have been times where I’ve just left when I really should have stayed, because I couldn’t take sitting there or standing there anymore.\n\nSo yeah, waiting my turn is hard. My mind just kind of wanders off before I mean for it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have trouble staying focused or feel restless in other settings, like when you’re at work or when you’re interacting with friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I’m not working right now, so I can’t really say like “at work” currently, but in other places, yes. It follows me around. It’s not just one place.\n\nThat’s honestly a big part of why I came in. I feel like I’ve got this motor running inside me all day. Like even if I’m sitting still on the outside, inside I don’t feel still. I feel like I need to get up, or move, or do something with myself. If I’m at church and I have to sit there for a long stretch, I start feeling trapped, like I’m stuck in the seat. I’ll be shifting around, thinking about standing up, looking for a reason to go do something, even if I know I’m supposed to just sit and listen.\n\nSame thing at the clinic, like waiting in a chair. I can sit there for a little bit, but then it starts building up in me. It’s not even that I’m trying to be rude or impatient. It just feels like my body is saying, “Get up, get up, get up.” And at home it happens too, which is what makes it frustrating, because home is where you’re supposed to relax. But I’ll be sitting there and still feel like I can’t settle, like I need to pop up and do something, even if there’s nothing that really needs doing.\n\nSo yeah, around people, out places, or even just at home by myself, that restless feeling is there a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others when they’re talking, acting without thinking about the consequences, or having trouble waiting your turn in situations like conversations or lines?\n\nPatient: Yeah… waiting my turn is a big one for me. Lines, especially, or sitting at the doctor’s office waiting to be called back — that gets me worked up fast.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Just stand here, just wait, everybody has to wait,” but my body doesn’t really listen. I start stepping around, shifting from one foot to the other, looking around, checking the door, watching who gets called before me. And then I’ll start asking, like, “How much longer do you think it’ll be?” or “Am I next?” even when I know they probably already told me to wait.\n\nIt’s embarrassing sometimes because I know I look impatient. I’m not trying to make anybody’s job harder. It just builds up in me, like I feel stuck there. At the doctor’s office, if I’m waiting too long, I’ll get up and walk around, go outside for a minute, come back in, ask again. And there have been times where I’ve just left when I really should have stayed, because I couldn’t take sitting there or standing there anymore.\n\nSo yeah, waiting my turn is hard. It’s not just annoying to me — it makes me feel all stirred up inside, like I have to do something right then.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often find yourself fidgeting, unable to relax when you need to be quiet, or talking more than you intend to, even when it’s not appropriate?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 36 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Fidgeting\nmanifestation: \"Even when I’m trying to be respectful, my leg is bouncing, my fingers are tapping the chair, or I’m twisting something in my hands the whole time.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, the fidgeting part for sure. Even when I’m trying to be respectful — like I know I’m supposed to sit still and be quiet — my body is still doing something.

My leg will be bouncing without me even deciding to do it. Or I’ll be tapping my fingers on the chair, or rubbing my hands together, or twisting something in my hands if I’ve got anything nearby. Sometimes I don’t even notice at first until somebody looks at me, or I hear the tapping myself and realize I’ve been doing it the whole time.

It’s like I can tell myself, “Just sit still, don’t move around so much,” but then a minute later my foot’s going again, or my fingers are picking at something. I don’t mean it to be distracting or disrespectful. I really don’t. It just feels like if I have to be quiet, some part of me still has to move.

Can you tell me what's been bothering you or what brings you here today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I came in because I just can’t seem to be still. It’s like I got a motor running inside me all day, like my body is always telling me to move, even when I’m supposed to be sitting quiet.\n\nLike at church, I try to sit there and listen, but after a little bit I feel trapped in the seat. Do you also feel this way in other places, like at work or school, or when you're out with friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I notice it most in places where I’m expected to sit still, like church, here at the clinic, or even at home when I’m supposed to just sit down and relax. It’s like my body doesn’t know how to just be quiet. Same kind of thing here at the clinic, just waiting, or even at home where there’s no reason I should feel worked up. I’ll be sitting there and it’s like there’s a motor inside me that won’t shut off, and I have to get up, walk around, or do something with my hands.\n\nSo it’s not only when I’m anxious. It’s just there a lot of the time, like my body doesn’t know how to be still.\n\nDoctor: Do you also feel a sense of inner tension or mental restlessness, like your mind is racing or you just can’t relax inside, even if you’re not moving?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s hard for me to separate it, you know? Because even if I’m sitting still on the outside for a minute, inside I still feel like I got that motor running. Like my body might be in the chair, but I don’t feel settled.\n\nAt church, for example, I’ll be sitting there trying to be respectful and listen, and I can feel this thing building in me, like I’m trapped in the seat. Same here at the clinic, sitting and waiting, I start feeling like I need to get up, move around, touch something, do something with my hands. Even at home, where I should be able to just sit down and be comfortable, I still get that feeling like I can’t stay put.\n\nSo yeah, even before I move, there’s this restless feeling inside me. Like I’m wound up, and if I don’t get up or do something, it just gets more uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually excited, energized, or like your thoughts were racing so fast you couldn’t keep up with them?\n\nPatient: Um… unusually excited or energized? I don’t know if I’d call it that. I mean, I get restless and wound up, like I’ve been saying, but not really like I’m in some great mood or full of big energy where I feel different from myself.\n\nAs for thoughts racing… sometimes my mind doesn’t feel settled, but I’m not sure it’s like my thoughts are going so fast I can’t keep up with them. It’s more like I can’t relax, you know? Like I feel uncomfortable sitting there. But “unusually excited” or my thoughts flying that fast… I can’t really say I’ve noticed that, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus or concentrate on things, like reading, watching TV, or following conversations, even when you're not moving around?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even if I’m sitting still and not moving around, I can have a hard time keeping with what somebody’s saying. Like I’ll start out listening good, I really will, and I’ll be trying to follow it, but then somewhere in the middle my mind just kind of slips off.\n\nIt happens with regular stuff I need to know, too. Like if somebody’s talking through ride times — who’s picking up who, what time we’re leaving, where I’m supposed to be — I’ll hear the first part, and then I miss a piece, and then I’m confused later. Same with pantry lists, like if someone’s telling me what needs to be picked up or what’s already there, I’ll think I got it, but then I realize I missed some of it. Or church plans, when people are explaining what’s going on, what time something starts, who’s doing what… I’m listening at first, but after a bit I lose pieces I really needed.\n\nAnd it’s frustrating because it’s not always like I’m walking around or doing something else. I can be right there looking at the person, trying to listen, and still it doesn’t all stay with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find that you’re not paying close attention to details, or do you make careless mistakes because your mind is somewhere else?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure about that one. I mean, I can say I miss things sometimes, but whether it’s because I’m not paying close attention to details or because my mind is somewhere else… I don’t know if I could separate that out real clear.\n\nLike, if somebody later says, “You didn’t notice that part,” or “That’s not what I said,” I might realize I missed something, but I don’t always know in the moment what happened. I don’t sit there thinking, “Oh, I’m ignoring the details.” It’s more after the fact that I might see I didn’t catch everything.\n\nAs far as careless mistakes, I don’t know. I don’t have a good example coming to mind right now. I’m not saying it never happens, but I haven’t really paid attention to it that way, like enough to say yes, that’s a big thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you also have trouble staying focused or feel restless in settings like work, school, or when you’re out with friends, even if you’re not moving around?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually kind of the main reason I came in, to be honest. It’s like I’ve got a motor running inside me all day. That’s the best way I can explain it. Even if I’m sitting there and I’m not supposed to be doing anything, inside I don’t feel still. I feel like I need to get up, or shift around, or go do something, or I’m just… stuck in my own skin, if that makes sense.\n\nLike at church, if I have to sit through everything, I start feeling trapped. Not because anything bad is happening, but because sitting there that long feels almost unbearable sometimes. Same thing at the clinic, like waiting around, sitting in the chair, trying to just be patient — I can do it for a little while, but then I start feeling like I’ve got to stand up or move or find some reason to do something.\n\nAnd at home too, it’s not like it only happens out in public. If I’m just sitting there and I’m supposed to relax, I don’t really relax. I’ll feel that pressure inside, like I need to get up. Even if I’m not physically running around, I still feel restless inside. That’s probably the biggest thing — I don’t feel settled.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing these feelings of restlessness and trouble focusing? Were they present when you were a child, or did they start more recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it was definitely there when I was a kid. I don’t think this is something that just started recently. I mean, it feels worse now in some ways because I’m older and there’s more I’m supposed to handle, but when I think back, it was already happening when I was a boy in school.\n\nTeachers were always moving my seat around. Like, not just once or twice — it felt like they were always trying to put me somewhere else because I couldn’t stay put. I’d be up before class was done, or I’d jump out of my seat when I wasn’t supposed to. Sometimes I’d talk out of turn, like I knew I was supposed to wait but it would just come out anyway, and then I’d get in trouble for it.\n\nI remember being told to sit down, be quiet, stop moving, wait until class is over… that kind of thing. So, yeah, when you ask if it was there as a child, I’d say yes. It goes way back.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have trouble staying focused or feel restless when you’re doing tasks that require you to keep your attention on something for a while, like reading, writing, or working on a project?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It happens a lot when somebody is telling me something I’m supposed to hold onto for more than just a second.\n\nLike if we’re talking about ride times — say somebody’s telling me, “Be ready at this time, then we’re going here, then we’re picking up so-and-so,” I’ll be listening at the start. I’m not trying to ignore them. I’ll be looking at them, nodding, thinking I’m getting it. But then somewhere in the middle my mind kind of slips off, and I don’t always notice right away. Then later I realize I missed the part I actually needed, like what time exactly or where I was supposed to be.\n\nSame thing with pantry lists or church plans. If somebody’s going through a list of things, I might catch the first couple things, then it gets blurry. I’ll remember there was something about this person bringing that, or we’re supposed to meet somewhere, but I don’t have the full thing. Same thing at the clinic, like waiting around, sitting in the chair, trying to just be patient — I can do it for a little while, but then I start feeling like I’ve got to stand up or move or find some reason to do something.\n\nAnd at home too, it’s not like it only happens out in public. If I’m just sitting there and I’m supposed to relax, I don’t really relax. I’ll feel that pressure inside, like I need to get up. Even if I’m not physically running around, I still feel restless inside. That’s probably the biggest thing — I don’t feel settled.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing these feelings of restlessness and trouble focusing? Were they present when you were a child, or did they start more recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it was definitely there when I was a kid. I don’t think this is something that just started recently. I mean, it feels worse now in some ways because I’m older and there’s more I’m supposed to handle, but when I think back, it was already happening when I was a boy in school.\n\nTeachers were always moving my seat around. Like, not just once or twice — it felt like they were always trying to put me somewhere else because I couldn’t stay put. I’d be up before class was done, or I’d jump out of my seat when I wasn’t supposed to. Sometimes I’d talk out of turn, like I knew I was supposed to wait but it would just come out anyway, and then I’d get in trouble for it.\n\nI remember being told to sit down, be quiet, stop moving, wait until class is over… that kind of thing. So, yeah, when you ask if it was there as a child, I’d say yes. It goes way back.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have trouble staying focused or feel restless when you’re doing tasks that require you to keep your attention on something for a while, like reading, writing, or working on a project?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It happens a lot when somebody is telling me something I’m supposed to hold onto for more than just a second.\n\nLike if we’re talking about ride times — say somebody’s telling me, “Be ready at this time, then we’re going here, then we’re picking up so-and-so,” I’ll be listening at the start. I’m not trying to ignore them. I’ll be looking at them, nodding, thinking I’m getting it. But then somewhere in the middle my mind kind of slips off, and I don’t always notice right away. Then later I realize I missed the part I actually needed, like what time exactly or where I was supposed to be.\n\nSame thing with pantry lists or church plans. If somebody’s going through a list of things, I might catch the first couple things, then it gets blurry. I’ll remember there was something about this person bringing that, or we’re supposed to meet somewhere, but I don’t have the full thing. Then I have to ask again, and I feel bad because I know they already told me.\n\nSo yeah, if it’s something that needs me to keep my attention on it for a while, especially listening to instructions or plans, I start out okay but I don’t stay locked in. My mind just kind of wanders off before I mean for it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have trouble staying focused or feel restless in other settings, like when you’re at work or when you’re interacting with friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I’m not working right now, so I can’t really say like “at work” currently, but in other places, yes. It follows me around. It’s not just one place.\n\nThat’s honestly a big part of why I came in. I feel like I’ve got this motor running inside me all day. Like even if I’m sitting still on the outside, inside I don’t feel still. I feel like I need to get up, or move, or do something with myself. If I’m at church and I have to sit there for a long stretch, I start feeling trapped, like I’m stuck in the seat. I’ll be shifting around, thinking about standing up, looking for a reason to go do something, even if I know I’m supposed to just sit and listen.\n\nSame thing at the clinic, like waiting in a chair. I can sit there for a little bit, but then it starts building up in me. It’s not even that I’m trying to be rude or impatient. It just feels like my body is saying, “Get up, get up, get up.” And at home it happens too, which is what makes it frustrating, because home is where you’re supposed to relax. But I’ll be sitting there and still feel like I can’t settle, like I need to pop up and do something, even if there’s nothing that really needs doing.\n\nSo yeah, around people, out places, or even just at home by myself, that restless feeling is there a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others when they’re talking, acting without thinking about the consequences, or having trouble waiting your turn in situations like conversations or lines?\n\nPatient: Yeah… waiting my turn is a big one for me. Lines, especially, or sitting at the doctor’s office waiting to be called back — that gets me worked up fast.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Just stand here, just wait, everybody has to wait,” but my body doesn’t really listen. I start stepping around, shifting from one foot to the other, looking around, checking the door, watching who gets called before me. And then I’ll start asking, like, “How much longer do you think it’ll be?” or “Am I next?” even when I know they probably already told me to wait.\n\nIt’s embarrassing sometimes because I know I look impatient. I’m not trying to make anybody’s job harder. It just builds up in me, like I feel stuck there. At the doctor’s office, if I’m waiting too long, I’ll get up and walk around, go outside for a minute, come back in, ask again. And there have been times where I’ve just left when I really should have stayed, because I couldn’t take sitting there or standing there anymore.\n\nSo yeah, waiting my turn is hard. It’s not just annoying to me — it makes me feel all stirred up inside, like I have to do something right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting, unable to relax when you need to be quiet, or talking more than you intend to, even when it’s not appropriate?\n\nPatient: Yeah, the fidgeting part for sure. Even when I’m trying to be respectful — like I know I’m supposed to sit still and be quiet — my body is still doing something.\n\nMy leg will be bouncing without me even deciding to do it. Or I’ll be tapping my fingers on the chair, or rubbing my hands together, or twisting something in my hands if I’ve got anything nearby. Sometimes I don’t even notice at first until somebody looks at me, or I hear the tapping myself and realize I’ve been doing it the whole time.\n\nIt’s like I can tell myself, “Just sit still, don’t move around so much,” but then a minute later my foot’s going again, or my fingers are picking at something. I don’t mean it to be distracting or disrespectful. I really don’t. It just feels like if I have to be quiet, some part of me still has to move.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, that’s exactly when I notice it the most, honestly — when everybody else is calm and sitting still, and I’m supposed to be calm too.

That’s part of why I came in, because it feels like I’ve got this motor running inside me all day. Like my body is sitting there, but inside I’m not settled at all. If I’m somewhere quiet, like church, and people are sitting and listening, I start feeling trapped in the seat. I’ll shift around, lean forward, lean back, look around, try to make myself stay put, but the whole time I’m thinking, “I need to get up. I need to move. I need to do something.”

Same kind of thing happens at the clinic if I’m waiting. Everybody else might be just sitting there normal, reading something or looking at their phone, and I’m over there feeling like I can’t take being in the chair anymore. I’ll feel like I have to stand up or walk around, even if there’s no real reason to.

And even at home, which is frustrating, because there’s no pressure there really. I can be in my own place and still feel like I can’t just relax and sit still. I’ll sit down and then almost right away there’s this feeling like I need to pop up and do something, even if I don’t know what it is. It’s like being still makes me feel boxed in.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, thank you for coming in. Do you also feel this way in other places, like at work or school, or when you're out with friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I notice it most in places where I’m expected to sit still, like church, here at the clinic, or even at home when I’m supposed to just sit down and relax. It’s just there a lot of the time, like my body doesn’t know how to be still.\n\nDoctor: Do you also feel a sense of inner tension or mental restlessness, like your mind is racing or you just can’t relax inside, even if you’re not moving?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s hard for me to separate it, you know? Because even if I’m sitting still on the outside for a minute, inside I still feel like I got that motor running. Like my body might be in the chair, but I don’t feel settled.\n\nAt church, for example, I’ll be sitting there trying to be respectful and listen, and I can feel this thing building in me, like I’m trapped in the seat. Even at home, where I should be able to just sit down and be comfortable, I still get that feeling like I can’t stay put.\n\nSo yeah, even before I move, there’s this restless feeling inside me. Like I’m wound up, and if I don’t get up or do something, it just gets more uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually excited, energized, or like your thoughts were racing so fast you couldn’t keep up with them?\n\nPatient: Um… unusually excited or energized? I don’t know if I’d call it that. I mean, I get restless and wound up, like I’ve been saying, but not really like I’m in some great mood or full of big energy where I feel different from myself.\n\nAs for thoughts racing… sometimes my mind doesn’t feel settled, but I’m not sure it’s like my thoughts are going so fast I can’t keep up with them. It’s more like I can’t relax, you know? Like I feel uncomfortable sitting there. But “unusually excited” or my thoughts flying that fast… I can’t really say I’ve noticed that, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus or concentrate on things, like reading, watching TV, or following conversations, even when you're not moving around?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even if I’m sitting still and not moving around, I can have a hard time keeping with what somebody’s saying. Like I’ll start out listening good, I really will, and I’ll be trying to follow it, but then somewhere in the middle my mind just kind of slips off.\n\nIt happens with regular stuff I need to know, too. Like if somebody’s talking through ride times — who’s picking up who, what time we’re leaving, where I’m supposed to be — I’ll hear the first part, and then I miss a piece, and then I’m confused later. Same with pantry lists, like if someone’s telling me what needs to be picked up or what’s already there, I’ll think I got it, but then I realize I missed some of it. Or church plans, when people are explaining what’s going on, what time something starts, who’s doing what… I’m listening at first, but after a bit I lose pieces I really needed.\n\nAnd it’s frustrating because it’s not always like I’m walking around or doing something else. I can be right there looking at the person, trying to listen, and still it doesn’t all stay with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find that you’re not paying close attention to details, or do you make careless mistakes because your mind is somewhere else?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure about that one. I mean, I can say I miss things sometimes, but whether it’s because I’m not paying close attention to details or because my mind is somewhere else… I don’t know if I could separate that out real clear.\n\nLike, if somebody later says, “You didn’t notice that part,” or “That’s not what I said,” I might realize I missed something, but I don’t always know in the moment what happened. I don’t sit there thinking, “Oh, I’m ignoring the details.” It’s more after the fact that I might see I didn’t catch everything.\n\nAs far as careless mistakes, I don’t know. I don’t have a good example coming to mind right now. I’m not saying it never happens, but I haven’t really paid attention to it that way, like enough to say yes, that’s a big thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you also have trouble staying focused or feel restless in settings like work, school, or when you’re out with friends, even if you’re not moving around?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually kind of the main reason I came in, to be honest. It’s like I’ve got a motor running inside me all day. That’s the best way I can explain it. Even if I’m sitting there and I’m not supposed to be doing anything, inside I don’t feel still. I feel like I need to get up, or shift around, or go do something, or I’m just… stuck in my own skin, if that makes sense.\n\nLike at church, if I have to sit through everything, I start feeling trapped. Not because anything bad is happening, but because sitting there that long feels almost unbearable sometimes. Same thing at the clinic, like waiting around, sitting in the chair, trying to just be patient — I can do it for a little while, but then I start feeling like I’ve got to stand up or move or find some reason to do something.\n\nAnd at home too, it’s not like it only happens out in public. If I’m just sitting there and I’m supposed to relax, I don’t really relax. I’ll feel that pressure inside, like I need to get up. Even if I’m not physically running around, I still feel restless inside. That’s probably the biggest thing — I don’t feel settled.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing these feelings of restlessness and trouble focusing? Were they present when you were a child, or did they start more recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it was definitely there when I was a kid. I don’t think this is something that just started recently. I mean, it feels worse now in some ways because I’m older and there’s more I’m supposed to handle, but when I think back, it was already happening when I was a boy in school.\n\nTeachers were always moving my seat around. Like, not just once or twice — it felt like they were always trying to put me somewhere else because I couldn’t stay put. I’d be up before class was done, or I’d jump out of my seat when I wasn’t supposed to. Sometimes I’d talk out of turn, like I knew I was supposed to wait but it would just come out anyway, and then I’d get in trouble for it.\n\nI remember being told to sit down, be quiet, stop moving, wait until class is over… that kind of thing. So, yeah, when you ask if it was there as a child, I’d say yes. It goes way back.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have trouble staying focused or feel restless when you’re doing tasks that require you to keep your attention on something for a while, like reading, writing, or working on a project?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It happens a lot when somebody is telling me something I’m supposed to hold onto for more than just a second.\n\nLike if we’re talking about ride times — say somebody’s telling me, “Be ready at this time, then we’re going here, then we’re picking up so-and-so,” I’ll be listening at the start. I’m not trying to ignore them. I’ll be looking at them, nodding, thinking I’m getting it. But then somewhere in the middle my mind kind of slips off, and I don’t always notice right away. Then later I realize I missed the part I actually needed, like what time exactly or where I was supposed to be.\n\nSame thing with pantry lists or church plans. If somebody’s going through a list of things, I might catch the first couple things, then it gets blurry. I’ll remember there was something about this person bringing that, or we’re supposed to meet somewhere, but I don’t have the full thing. Then I have to ask again, and I feel bad because I know they already told me.\n\nSo yeah, if it’s something that needs me to keep my attention on it for a while, especially listening to instructions or plans, I start out okay but I don’t stay locked in. My mind just kind of wanders off before I mean for it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have trouble staying focused or feel restless in other settings, like when you’re at work or when you’re interacting with friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, I’m not working right now, so I can’t really say like “at work” currently, but in other places, yes. It follows me around. It’s not just one place.\n\nThat’s honestly a big part of why I came in. I feel like I’ve got this motor running inside me all day. Like even if I’m sitting still on the outside, inside I don’t feel still. I feel like I need to get up, or move, or do something with myself. If I’m at church and I have to sit there for a long stretch, I start feeling trapped, like I’m stuck in the seat. I’ll be shifting around, thinking about standing up, looking for a reason to go do something, even if I know I’m supposed to just sit and listen.\n\nSame thing at the clinic, like waiting in a chair. I can sit there for a little bit, but then it starts building up in me. It’s not even that I’m trying to be rude or impatient. It just feels like my body is saying, “Get up, get up, get up.” And at home it happens too, which is what makes it frustrating, because home is where you’re supposed to relax. But I’ll be sitting there and still feel like I can’t settle, like I need to pop up and do something, even if there’s nothing that really needs doing.\n\nSo yeah, around people, out places, or even just at home by myself, that restless feeling is there a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others when they’re talking, acting without thinking about the consequences, or having trouble waiting your turn in situations like conversations or lines?\n\nPatient: Yeah… waiting my turn is a big one for me. Lines, especially, or sitting at the doctor’s office waiting to be called back — that gets me worked up fast.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Just stand here, just wait, everybody has to wait,” but my body doesn’t really listen. I start stepping around, shifting from one foot to the other, looking around, checking the door, watching who gets called before me. And then I’ll start asking, like, “How much longer do you think it’ll be?” or “Am I next?” even when I know they probably already told me to wait.\n\nIt’s embarrassing sometimes because I know I look impatient. I’m not trying to make anybody’s job harder. It just builds up in me, like I feel stuck there. At the doctor’s office, if I’m waiting too long, I’ll get up and walk around, go outside for a minute, come back in, ask again. And there have been times where I’ve just left when I really should have stayed, because I couldn’t take sitting there or standing there anymore.\n\nSo yeah, waiting my turn is hard. It’s not just annoying to me — it makes me feel all stirred up inside, like I have to do something right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting, unable to relax when you need to be quiet, or talking more than you intend to, even when it’s not appropriate?\n\nPatient: Yeah, the fidgeting part for sure. Even when I’m trying to be respectful — like I know I’m supposed to sit still and be quiet — my body is still doing something.\n\nMy leg will be bouncing without me even deciding to do it. Or I’ll be tapping my fingers on the chair, or rubbing my hands together, or twisting something in my hands if I’ve got anything nearby. Sometimes I don’t even notice at first until somebody looks at me, or I hear the tapping myself and realize I’ve been doing it the whole time.\n\nIt’s like I can tell myself, “Just sit still, don’t move around so much,” but then a minute later my foot’s going again, or my fingers are picking at something. I don’t mean it to be distracting or disrespectful. I really don’t. It just feels like if I have to be quiet, some part of me still has to move.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself more restless or unable to stay still in situations where other people are calm and quiet, like in meetings, at the movies, or during meals?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s exactly when I notice it the most, honestly — when everybody else is calm and sitting still, and I’m supposed to be calm too.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in, because it feels like I’ve got this motor running inside me all day. Like my body is sitting there, but inside I’m not settled at all. If I’m somewhere quiet, like church, and people are sitting and listening, I start feeling trapped in the seat. I’ll shift around, lean forward, lean back, look around, try to make myself stay put, but the whole time I’m thinking, “I need to get up. I need to move. I need to do something.”\n\nSame kind of thing happens at the clinic if I’m waiting. Everybody else might be just sitting there normal, reading something or looking at their phone, and I’m over there feeling like I can’t take being in the chair anymore. I’ll feel like I have to stand up or walk around, even if there’s no real reason to.\n\nAnd even at home, which is frustrating, because there’s no pressure there really. I can be in my own place and still feel like I can’t just relax and sit still. I’ll sit down and then almost right away there’s this feeling like I need to pop up and do something, even if I don’t know what it is. It’s like being still makes me feel boxed in.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{
  "candidates": ["F90.0", "F90.8", "F90.9"],
  "note": "",
  "is_final": false
}

[
  {
