========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yeah, that happens to me a lot, especially with talking. I’ll jump in with an answer before the person is even finished asking or explaining something.

Like at church, if the pastor is saying something or asking something, I’ll think I know where he’s going with it, and I’ll blurt something out too soon. Then he finishes and I realize, oh… that wasn’t even what he was asking, or I answered the wrong thing. And then I feel stupid, because everybody heard me jump in like that.

It happens with my cousin too. He’ll be telling me something, and before he gets the whole sentence out I’m already answering him, like I can’t just wait for the last part. Then sometimes he gets irritated and says, “Man, let me finish,” and I know he’s right. I don’t mean to cut him off. It’s like the answer comes out before I can stop it.

Even at the pantry, with the lady there, she’ll be explaining what I need to do or asking me a question, and I’ll answer before she’s done. Then I realize I answered the wrong thing, or I made her mad because it sounded like I wasn’t listening. I am trying to listen, but I jump ahead in my head and my mouth just goes with it.

And then afterward I’m standing there thinking, why didn’t I just wait two more seconds? But in the moment it feels like it comes out too fast for me to catch it.

Lines, especially, or sitting at the doctor’s office waiting to be called back — that gets me worked up fast.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Just stand here, just wait, everybody has to wait,” but my body doesn’t really listen. I start stepping around, shifting from one foot to the other, looking around, checking the door, watching who gets called before me. And then I’ll start asking, like, “How much longer do you think it’ll be?” or “Am I next?” even when I know they probably already told me to wait.\n\nIt’s embarrassing sometimes because I know I look impatient. I’m not trying to make anybody’s job harder. It just builds up in me, like I feel stuck there. At the doctor’s office, if I’m waiting too long, I’ll get up and walk around, go outside for a minute, come back in, ask again. And there have been times where I’ve just left when I really should have stayed, because I couldn’t take sitting there or standing there anymore.\n\nSo yeah, waiting my turn is hard. It’s not just annoying to me — it makes me feel all stirred up inside, like I have to do something right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting, unable to relax when you need to be quiet, or talking more than you intend to, even when it’s not appropriate?\n\nPatient: Yeah, the fidgeting part for sure. Even when I’m trying to be respectful — like I know I’m supposed to sit still and be quiet — my body is still doing something.\n\nMy leg will be bouncing without me even deciding to do it. Or I’ll be tapping my fingers on the chair, or rubbing my hands together, or twisting something in my hands if I’ve got anything nearby. Sometimes I don’t even notice at first until somebody looks at me, or I hear the tapping myself and realize I’ve been doing it the whole time.\n\nIt’s like I can tell myself, “Just sit still, don’t move around so much,” but then a minute later my foot’s going again, or my fingers are picking at something. I don’t mean it to be distracting or disrespectful. I really don’t. It just feels like if I have to be quiet, some part of me still has to move.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself more restless or unable to stay still in situations where other people are calm and quiet, like in meetings, at the movies, or during meals?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s exactly when I notice it the most, honestly — when everybody else is calm and sitting still, and I’m supposed to be calm too.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in, because it feels like I’ve got this motor running inside me all day. Like my body is sitting there, but inside I’m not settled at all. If I’m somewhere quiet, like church, and people are sitting and listening, I start feeling trapped in the seat. I’ll shift around, lean forward, lean back, look around, try to make myself stay put, but the whole time I’m thinking, “I need to get up. I need to move. I need to do something.”\n\nSame kind of thing happens at the clinic if I’m waiting. Everybody else might be just sitting there normal, reading something or looking at their phone, and I’m over there feeling like I can’t take being in the chair anymore. I’ll feel like I have to stand up or walk around, even if there’s no real reason to.\n\nAnd even at home, which is frustrating, because there’s no pressure there really. I can be in my own place and still feel like I can’t just relax and sit still. I’ll sit down and then almost right away there’s this feeling like I need to pop up and do something, even if I don’t know what it is. It’s like being still makes me feel boxed in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself acting so quickly on urges that you don’t stop to think about the consequences, like saying or doing something before you mean to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that happens to me a lot, especially with talking. I’ll jump in with an answer before the person is even finished asking or explaining something.\n\nLike at church, if the pastor is saying something or asking something, I’ll think I know where he’s going with it, and I’ll blurt something out too soon. Then he finishes and I realize, oh… that wasn’t even what he was asking, or I answered the wrong thing. And then I feel stupid, because everybody heard me jump in like that.\n\nIt happens with my cousin too. He’ll be telling me something, and before he gets the whole sentence out I’m already answering him, like I can’t just wait for the last part. Then sometimes he gets irritated and says, “Man, let me finish,” and I know he’s right. I don’t mean to cut him off. It’s like the answer comes out before I can stop it.\n\nEven at the pantry, with the lady there, she’ll be explaining what I need to do or asking me a question, and I’ll answer before she’s done. Then I realize I answered the wrong thing, or I made her mad because it sounded like I wasn’t listening. I am trying to listen, but I jump ahead in my head and my mouth just goes with it.\n\nAnd then afterward I’m standing there thinking, why didn’t I just wait two more seconds? But in the moment it feels like it comes out too fast for me to catch it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I need to do something.”\n\nSame kind of thing happens at the clinic if I’m waiting. Everybody else might be just sitting there normal, reading something or looking at their phone, and I’m over there feeling like I can’t take being in the chair anymore. I’ll feel like I have to stand up or walk around, even if there’s no real reason to.\n\nAnd even at home, which is frustrating, because there’s no pressure there really. I can be in my own place and still feel like I can’t just relax and sit still. I’ll sit down and then almost right away there’s this feeling like I need to pop up and do something, even if I don’t know what it is. It’s like being still makes me feel boxed in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself acting so quickly on urges that you don’t stop to think about the consequences, like saying or doing something before you mean to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that happens to me a lot, especially with talking. I’ll jump in with an answer before the person is even finished asking or explaining something.\n\nLike at church, if the pastor is saying something or asking something, I’ll think I know where he’s going with it, and I’ll blurt something out too soon. Then he finishes and I realize, oh… that wasn’t even what he was asking, or I answered the wrong thing. And then I feel stupid, because everybody heard me jump in like that.\n\nIt happens with my cousin too. He’ll be telling me something, and before he gets the whole sentence out I’m already answering him, like I can’t just wait for the last part. I am trying to listen, but I jump ahead in my head and my mouth just goes with it.\n\nAnd then afterward I’m standing there thinking, why didn’t I just wait two more seconds? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… that happens with instructions a lot. Like I’ll think I understand what somebody wants me to do, so I’ll just start moving on it before I really got the whole thing.

If a relative asks me to run an errand, for example, I might hear the first part — like go pick this up, or drop this off — and I’ll go do that part. But there was a second part or another thing they told me, and by the time I’m out doing it, it’s just gone from my head. Then later they’ll ask, “Did you also do the other thing?” and I’m like… no, I didn’t. I’ll realize I never finished the whole errand, just the first piece of it.

Same thing at the pantry sometimes. Somebody will tell me what job they need done, and I’ll get started because I don’t want to look like I’m just standing around. But I’ll do the first thing and then kind of miss the rest of what was expected. Then later somebody notices, like, “Hey, this didn’t get finished,” or “You were supposed to do this after that,” and that’s when it comes out that I only did part of it.

It’s embarrassing because I’m not trying to be lazy or ignore people. In my mind, I started it, so I feel like I was doing what I was supposed to do. But then everybody else sees that I didn’t actually finish the whole thing. And I hate that, because it makes me seem careless, but a lot of the time I just move too fast at the beginning and don’t hold onto all the instructions.

Like I’ll think I understand what somebody wants me to do, so I’ll just start moving on it before I really got the whole thing.\n\nIf a relative asks me to run an errand, for example, I might hear the first part — like go pick this up, or drop this off — and I’ll go do that part. But there was a second part or another thing they told me, and by the time I’m out doing it, it’s just gone from my head. Then later they’ll ask, “Did you also do the other thing?” and I’m like… no, I didn’t. I’ll realize I never finished the whole errand, just the first piece of it.\n\nSame thing at the pantry sometimes. Somebody will tell me what job they need done, and I’ll get started because I don’t want to look like I’m just standing around. But I’ll do the first thing and then kind of miss the rest of what was expected. Then later somebody notices, like, “Hey, this didn’t get finished,” or “You were supposed to do this after that,” and that’s when it comes out that I only did part of it.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I’m not trying to be lazy or ignore people. In my mind, I started it, so I feel like I was doing what I was supposed to do. But then everybody else sees that I didn’t actually finish the whole thing. And I hate that, because it makes me seem careless, but a lot of the time I just move too fast at the beginning and don’t hold onto all the instructions.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah… it really does. I mean, that’s probably the part that bothers me the most, because it’s not just me feeling uncomfortable inside. It’s like it spills over onto everybody around me, and then people stop depending on me.

At church, at the pantry, even in my own family, people don’t really trust me with certain things anymore. Like rides, appointments, picking things up, bringing supplies — stuff where somebody needs me to be on time, remember what I’m supposed to do, and follow through. And I get why they feel that way, I do, because there have been times I meant to help and then I messed it up somehow. But it still hurts.

It’s embarrassing because I’ll say, “Yeah, I can do that,” and I really mean it in the moment. I’m not trying to blow anybody off or make things harder. But then something gets missed, or I don’t handle it the way they expected, and after enough times they start giving those jobs to somebody else. Or they’ll double-check me like they already expect I’m going to forget or mess it up. That makes me feel like a grown man who can’t be counted on.

And with my family, that’s probably the worst feeling. I want to be useful. I want them to feel like, “Okay, he’s got it.” But instead I feel like I keep letting everybody down, even when my intention is to help. So yeah, it affects my day-to-day life a lot. It affects how people see me, and honestly how I see myself too.
